<title>1TabPFN-3.5 against the strongest non-TabPFN competitor on each benchmark. Rank is the position of the best TabPFN-3.5 member (TabPFN-3.5-Thinking on the first four benchmarks, TabPFN-Rel (3.5) on RelArena- α , TabPFN-3.5 on TALENT and ScoringBench) among the methods on the benchmark’s leaderboard. Win rates are the share of splits won against the best other Tabular Foundation Model (TFM) and the best non-foundation model, datasets weighted equally. Details in .</title>

<script>
  // Restore the saved color scheme preference, or
  // enact the browser preference if "automatic", 
  // without expecting DOM load to have completed.
  //
  // Also restore any saved readingmode and ToC display preferences.
  function initializeReadingPreferences() {
    let saved_theme = localStorage.getItem("ar5iv_theme") || "automatic";
    if (saved_theme === "automatic") {
      if (window.matchMedia("(prefers-color-scheme: dark)").matches) {
        saved_theme = "dark";
      }
    }
    if (saved_theme == "dark") {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "dark");
    } else {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "light");
    }

    const tocDisplay = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_toc_display');
    if (tocDisplay) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-toc-display", tocDisplay);
    }
    const readingMode = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_reading_mode');
    if (readingMode) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-reading-mode", readingMode);
    }
    // Pre-apply spinout-banner dismissal here, before the banner paints, so it
    // never flashes in only to be hidden later by the deferred arxiv-header.js.
    // Key matches arxiv-header.js: "arxiv-banner-dismissed:" + data-banner-name.
    if (localStorage.getItem('arxiv-banner-dismissed:spinout-nonprofit')) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-banner-dismissed", "");
    }
  }
  // Run as soon as JS starts, to minimize repainting
  initializeReadingPreferences();
</script>

<div id="p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent"><span class="ltx_inline-block"><svg id="p1.pic1" class="ltx_picture" height="722.43" overflow="visible" version="1.1" viewBox="0 0 650 722.43" width="650"><g style="--ltx-stroke-color:#000000;--ltx-fill-color:#000000;" fill="#000000" stroke="#000000" stroke-width="0.4pt" transform="translate(0,722.43) matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 0)"><g style="--ltx-fill-color:#D9D9D9;" fill="#D9D9D9" fill-opacity="1.0"><path style="stroke:none" d="M 0 8.99 L 0 713.43 C 0 718.4 4.03 722.43 8.99 722.43 L 641.01 722.43 C 645.97 722.43 650 718.4 650 713.43 L 650 8.99 C 650 4.03 645.97 0 641.01 0 L 8.99 0 C 4.03 0 0 4.03 0 8.99 Z"></path></g><g style="--ltx-fill-color:#F7F8FA;" fill="#F7F8FA" fill-opacity="1.0"><path style="stroke:none" d="M 0.69 8.99 L 0.69 713.43 C 0.69 718.02 4.41 721.74 8.99 721.74 L 641.01 721.74 C 645.59 721.74 649.31 718.02 649.31 713.43 L 649.31 8.99 C 649.31 4.41 645.59 0.69 641.01 0.69 L 8.99 0.69 C 4.41 0.69 0.69 4.41 0.69 8.99 Z"></path></g><g fill-opacity="1.0" transform="matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 26.77 27.08)"><foreignObject style="--ltx-fo-width:43.11em;--ltx-fo-height:48.52em;--ltx-fo-depth:0.22em;font-size:10pt;" height="674.43" overflow="visible" transform="matrix(1 0 0 -1 0 671.36)" width="596.52"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_container"><span class="ltx_foreignobject_content"><span id="p1.pic1.1" class="ltx_inline-logical-block ltx_minipage ltx_align_bottom" style="width:43.11em;">
<span id="p1.pic1.p4" class="ltx_para"><img src="2609.17895v2/figures/prior-logo.png" id="p1.pic1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_landscape" style="aspect-ratio:119/39;" width="119" height="39" alt="[Uncaptioned image]">
<span id="p1.pic1.p4.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="p1.pic1.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:173%;--ltx-fg-color:#000000;">TabPFN-3.5: Technical Report</span></span>
<span id="p1.pic1.p4.2" class="ltx_p"><a href="#A1" title="Appendix A Contributors" class="ltx_ref" style="font-size:120%;--ltx-fg-color:#000000;">Prior Labs Team</a><span id="p1.pic1.p4.2.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#000000;"> (see Appendix <a href="#A1" title="Appendix A Contributors" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A</span></a> for the list of contributors)</span></span>
<span id="p1.pic1.p4.3" class="ltx_p"><span id="p1.pic1.p4.3.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#000000;">We introduce <span id="p1.pic1.p4.3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">TabPFN-3.5</span>, our new flagship Tabular Foundation Model. It significantly outperforms its predecessor, <span id="p1.pic1.p4.3.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib1" title="" class="ltx_ref">1</a>]</cite>, and all existing baselines across a broad range of tabular problems. <span id="p1.pic1.p4.3.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> sets a new state of the art on standard tabular prediction in TabArena <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib2" title="" class="ltx_ref">2</a>]</cite>, and extends it to the data practitioners encounter in practice: non-i.i.d. data with temporal or grouped splits <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib3" title="" class="ltx_ref">3</a>]</cite>, tables with strings <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib4" title="" class="ltx_ref">4</a>]</cite>, text and images <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib5" title="" class="ltx_ref">5</a>]</cite>, high-cardinality categorical features, and wide tables with many features <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib3" title="" class="ltx_ref">3</a>]</cite>. These gains carry over to our task-specific harnesses: state of the art on relational data <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib6" title="" class="ltx_ref">6</a>]</cite> and stronger time-series forecasting <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib7" title="" class="ltx_ref">7</a>]</cite>. For faster inference, our variant <span id="p1.pic1.p4.3.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">TabPFN-3.5-Fast</span> runs up to <math id="p1.pic1.m1" class="ltx_math_unparsed" alttext="3\times" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mn style="--ltx-fg-color:#000000;" mathcolor="#000000">3</mn><mo style="--ltx-fg-color:#000000;" lspace="0.222em" mathcolor="#000000">×</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">3\times</annotation></semantics></math> faster than <span id="p1.pic1.p4.3.1.5" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> while keeping most of the accuracy gains. In addition, we upgrade <span id="p1.pic1.p4.3.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">TabPFN-3.5-Plus</span>, expanding our multimodal capabilities with advanced text and date handling alongside proprietary inference optimizations. Finally, we release a new version of our Thinking mode, <span id="p1.pic1.p4.3.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">TabPFN-3.5-Thinking</span>, which scales inference-time computation to push the state of the art further. It benefits from our stronger base model and from inference-time improvements that make it up to <math id="p1.pic1.m2" class="ltx_math_unparsed" alttext="12\times" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mn style="--ltx-fg-color:#000000;" mathcolor="#000000">12</mn><mo style="--ltx-fg-color:#000000;" lspace="0.222em" mathcolor="#000000">×</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">12\times</annotation></semantics></math> faster than <span id="p1.pic1.p4.3.1.8" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span>-Thinking.</span></span>
</span>
<span id="p1.pic1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<span id="p1.pic1.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="p1.pic1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps" style="font-size:90%;--ltx-fg-color:#737373;">Date</span><span id="p1.pic1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#000000;"> September 14th 2026</span></span>
</span>
<span id="p1.pic1.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<span id="p1.pic1.p2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="p1.pic1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps" style="font-size:90%;--ltx-fg-color:#737373;">License</span><span id="p1.pic1.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#000000;"> Code: Apache 2.0; weights: TABPFN-3.5 License v1.0 (see </span><a href="#S4" title="4 License and Availability" class="ltx_ref" style="--ltx-fg-color:#000000;"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a><span id="p1.pic1.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#000000;">)</span></span>
</span>
<span id="p1.pic1.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<span id="p1.pic1.p3.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="p1.pic1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps" style="font-size:90%;--ltx-fg-color:#737373;">Docs</span><span id="p1.pic1.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#000000;"> </span><a href="https://docs.priorlabs.ai" title="" class="ltx_ref ltx_url ltx_font_typewriter" style="--ltx-fg-color:#000000;">https://docs.priorlabs.ai</a></span>
</span></span></span></span></foreignObject></g></g></svg></span>
</div>

Win rates are the share of splits won against the best other Tabular Foundation Model (TFM) and the best non-foundation model, datasets weighted equally. Details in <a href="#A3.SS8" title="C.8 Details on the benchmark overview table ‣ Appendix C More Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.8</span></a>.</span></span></figcaption>

<section id="S1" class="ltx_section">
<h2 class="ltx_title ltx_title_section"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">1 </span>Introduction</h2>

<div id="S1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S1.p1.1" class="ltx_p">Tabular Foundation Models (TFMs) have replaced gradient-boosted trees, for decades the default choice for tabular prediction that drives decision making across science and industry <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib8" title="" class="ltx_ref">8</a>, <a href="#bib.bib9" title="" class="ltx_ref">9</a>, <a href="#bib.bib10" title="" class="ltx_ref">10</a>, <a href="#bib.bib11" title="" class="ltx_ref">11</a>, <a href="#bib.bib12" title="" class="ltx_ref">12</a>, <a href="#bib.bib13" title="" class="ltx_ref">13</a>]</cite>, as the strongest predictors on standard i.i.d. benchmarks: on TabArena <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib2" title="" class="ltx_ref">2</a>]</cite>, TFMs dominate the leaderboard, and the strongest default TFM outperforms AutoML systems that tune and ensemble models for hours <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib2" title="" class="ltx_ref">2</a>, <a href="#bib.bib14" title="" class="ltx_ref">14</a>]</cite>. Successive TabPFN releases <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib15" title="" class="ltx_ref">15</a>, <a href="#bib.bib16" title="" class="ltx_ref">16</a>, <a href="#bib.bib17" title="" class="ltx_ref">17</a>, <a href="#bib.bib1" title="" class="ltx_ref">1</a>]</cite> established and extended this paradigm, from a thousand clean numerical rows <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib15" title="" class="ltx_ref">15</a>]</cite> to a million rows with heterogeneous features (categorical and numerical), missing values, and many-class targets <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib16" title="" class="ltx_ref">16</a>, <a href="#bib.bib17" title="" class="ltx_ref">17</a>, <a href="#bib.bib1" title="" class="ltx_ref">1</a>]</cite>. Around these models, an active ecosystem has grown across time-series forecasting <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib18" title="" class="ltx_ref">18</a>, <a href="#bib.bib19" title="" class="ltx_ref">19</a>]</cite>, causal inference <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib20" title="" class="ltx_ref">20</a>, <a href="#bib.bib21" title="" class="ltx_ref">21</a>, <a href="#bib.bib22" title="" class="ltx_ref">22</a>]</cite>, graph learning <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib23" title="" class="ltx_ref">23</a>, <a href="#bib.bib24" title="" class="ltx_ref">24</a>]</cite>, relational data <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib6" title="" class="ltx_ref">6</a>]</cite>, interpretability <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib25" title="" class="ltx_ref">25</a>, <a href="#bib.bib26" title="" class="ltx_ref">26</a>]</cite>, survival analysis <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib27" title="" class="ltx_ref">27</a>, <a href="#bib.bib28" title="" class="ltx_ref">28</a>]</cite>, Bayesian optimization <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib29" title="" class="ltx_ref">29</a>, <a href="#bib.bib30" title="" class="ltx_ref">30</a>]</cite>, and reinforcement learning <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib31" title="" class="ltx_ref">31</a>]</cite>, with hundreds of published applications and millions of downloads; the <span id="S1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> report <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib1" title="" class="ltx_ref">1</a>]</cite> surveys both. The field is maturing: within the last year, TabICLv2 <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib32" title="" class="ltx_ref">32</a>]</cite>, TabFM <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib33" title="" class="ltx_ref">33</a>]</cite>, EXAONE-Tabular <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib34" title="" class="ltx_ref">34</a>]</cite>, TabDPT-Turbo <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib35" title="" class="ltx_ref">35</a>]</cite>,
and SAP-RPT-1 <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib36" title="" class="ltx_ref">36</a>]</cite> have been released as competitive TFMs, and benchmarks have broadened from numerical i.i.d. data to non-i.i.d. <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib37" title="" class="ltx_ref">37</a>, <a href="#bib.bib3" title="" class="ltx_ref">3</a>]</cite>, string-valued <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib4" title="" class="ltx_ref">4</a>]</cite>, multimodal <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib5" title="" class="ltx_ref">5</a>]</cite>, and relational <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib6" title="" class="ltx_ref">6</a>]</cite> data, and to calibrated predictive distributions <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib38" title="" class="ltx_ref">38</a>]</cite>. These new benchmarks also show where TFMs still fall short. <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citet"><a href="#bib.bib3" title="" class="ltx_ref">Purucker et al. [3]</a></cite> show that TFMs excel on clean i.i.d. data, yet even the best of them fall behind tuned conventional models on grouped or temporal splits, on tables with many rows or many features, and on tables with text or high-cardinality features. This is the data practitioners face every day. And the TFMs that have pushed accuracy furthest have also grown larger and slower, which makes them harder to deploy.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S1.p2.1" class="ltx_p">To address these open problems, we introduce <span id="S1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span>. It extends the lead of TFMs from clean i.i.d. benchmarks to all of these regimes, on which it is now competitive with heavily tuned gradient-boosted trees and MLPs. We observe these gains across benchmarks: <span id="S1.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> sets a new state of the art on TabArena <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib2" title="" class="ltx_ref">2</a>]</cite>, BeyondArena <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib3" title="" class="ltx_ref">3</a>]</cite>, STRABLE <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib4" title="" class="ltx_ref">4</a>]</cite> for tabular data with strings, MulTaBench <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib5" title="" class="ltx_ref">5</a>]</cite> for multimodal tabular learning with text and images, ScoringBench <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib38" title="" class="ltx_ref">38</a>]</cite> for predictive distributions, and, through TabPFN-Rel, RelArena <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib6" title="" class="ltx_ref">6</a>]</cite> for relational data. At the same time, it retains what made <span id="S1.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> practical: training sets of up to one million rows, many-class classification, calibrated predictive distributions from a single forward pass, and cached inference in which the training rows are processed once and test rows are then scored in well under a second. <span id="S1.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span>’s gains come from revised per-cell encodings, doubling the model width while keeping the cache size unchanged, a single checkpoint trained jointly for classification and regression, and a synthetic prior tuned towards high-cardinality, wide, and grouped data. <a href="#S2" title="2 Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> quantifies these gains.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S1.p3.1" class="ltx_p">We release two checkpoints compatible with the open-source <a href="https://github.com/PriorLabs/TabPFN" title="" class="ltx_ref ltx_href ltx_font_typewriter">tabpfn</a> package: <span id="S1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> as the core model and <span id="S1.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Fast</span>, an alpha checkpoint for latency-sensitive applications that runs up to <math id="S1.p3.m1" class="ltx_math_unparsed" alttext="6\times" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mn>6</mn><mo lspace="0.222em">×</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">6\times</annotation></semantics></math> faster than <span id="S1.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> (and up to <math id="S1.p3.m2" class="ltx_math_unparsed" alttext="140\times" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mn>140</mn><mo lspace="0.222em">×</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">140\times</annotation></semantics></math> faster than TabFM)
with a small drop in accuracy while still matching the previous state of the art on these benchmarks.
The core model was designed to serve as the foundation of <span id="S1.p3.1.4" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Plus</span>, which expands our multimodal capabilities for text and date processing alongside proprietary inference optimizations, as well as <span id="S1.p3.1.5" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Thinking</span>, which also scales inference-time computation to deliver peak performance. <span id="S1.p3.1.6" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Thinking</span> is our most accurate model across all use cases and further strengthens grouped and temporal modeling. <span id="S1.p3.1.7" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Plus</span>, <span id="S1.p3.1.8" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Fast</span>, and <span id="S1.p3.1.9" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Thinking</span> are available through the <a href="https://platform.priorlabs.ai" title="" class="ltx_ref ltx_href">Prior Labs API</a>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S1.p4.1" class="ltx_p">The remainder of this report presents the experimental results (<a href="#S2" title="2 Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>); describes the architecture, preprocessing, inference characteristics, and synthetic prior of <span id="S1.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span>, as well as <span id="S1.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Plus</span> and its Thinking mode (<a href="#S3" title="3 Model Design and Inference" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>); and details licensing (<a href="#S4" title="4 License and Availability" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>). For installation and usage, see <a href="https://docs.priorlabs.ai/" title="" class="ltx_ref ltx_href">our documentation</a>.</p>
</div>

<td id="S1.T2.st1.3.8.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_right ltx_border_t"><math id="S1.T2.st1.m20" class="ltx_Math" alttext="1{,}000{,}000" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn mathsize="0.900em">1,000,000</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1{,}000{,}000</annotation></semantics></math></td>
<td id="S1.T2.st1.3.8.3" class="ltx_td ltx_align_right ltx_border_t"><math id="S1.T2.st1.m21" class="ltx_Math" alttext="6{,}000" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn mathsize="0.900em">6,000</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">6{,}000</annotation></semantics></math></td>
<td id="S1.T2.st1.3.8.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_t" colspan="2"><math id="S1.T2.st1.m22" class="ltx_Math" alttext="220" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn mathsize="0.900em">220</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">220</annotation></semantics></math><span id="S1.T2.st1.3.8.4.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> M</span></td></tr>
<tr id="S1.T2.st1.3.9" class="ltx_tr">

<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering" style="font-size:90%;"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">(a) </span><span id="S1.T2.st1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Overview of TabPFN releases</span>, with the largest recommended row and feature counts, and the models’ parameter counts for each release. These are recommended limits for strong performance, not computational limits. Increasing the number of estimators allows support for up to 20K features.</figcaption>

</section>
<section id="S2" class="ltx_section">
<h2 class="ltx_title ltx_title_section"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">2 </span>Experimental Results</h2>

<div id="S2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> improves performance across a wide range of settings:</p>
<ul id="S2.I1" class="ltx_itemize">
<li id="S2.I1.i1" class="ltx_item" style="list-style-type:none;"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span> 
<div id="S2.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.I1.i1.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S2.I1.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Standard tabular benchmarks.</span> On TabArena, <span id="S2.I1.i1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> ranks first, and together with <span id="S2.I1.i1.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Fast</span> and <span id="S2.I1.i1.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Thinking</span> dominates the Elo-versus-time Pareto frontier (<a href="#S2.SS1" title="2.1 TabArena ‣ 2 Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2.1</span></a>). It also ranks first on the 300-dataset TALENT benchmark (<a href="#A3.SS5" title="C.5 TALENT ‣ Appendix C More Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.5</span></a>).</p>
</div></li>
<li id="S2.I1.i2" class="ltx_item" style="list-style-type:none;"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span> 
<div id="S2.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.I1.i2.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S2.I1.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Diverse and non-i.i.d. data.</span> On BeyondArena, which spans grouped and temporal splits, tiny to million-row tables, and text and high-cardinality features, <span id="S2.I1.i2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> ranks first overall, and for every subset of non-large (<math id="S2.I1.i2.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="&lt;" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mo>&lt;</mo><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">&lt;</annotation></semantics></math>100K rows) datasets (<a href="#S2.SS2" title="2.2 BeyondArena ‣ 2 Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2.2</span></a>).</p>
</div></li>
<li id="S2.I1.i3" class="ltx_item" style="list-style-type:none;"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span> 
<div id="S2.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.I1.i3.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S2.I1.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Multimodal data.</span> <span id="S2.I1.i3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> ranks first on STRABLE, where tables carry string fields such as names, addresses and free-text notes (<a href="#S2.SS3" title="2.3 STRABLE ‣ 2 Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2.3</span></a>), and on MulTaBench, where tabular
columns are paired with text and image fields (<a href="#S2.SS4" title="2.4 MulTaBench ‣ 2 Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2.4</span></a>). <span id="S2.I1.i3.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Plus</span>, which processes text columns natively, and <span id="S2.I1.i3.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Thinking</span> extend the lead further on both benchmarks (<a href="#S3.SS4" title="3.4 TabPFN-3.5-Plus and Thinking mode ‣ 3 Model Design and Inference" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.4</span></a>).</p>
</div></li>
<li id="S2.I1.i4" class="ltx_item" style="list-style-type:none;"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span> 
<div id="S2.I1.i4.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.I1.i4.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S2.I1.i4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Predictive distributions.</span> Beyond point predictions, <span id="S2.I1.i4.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> produces better predictive distributions, ranking first on ScoringBench, which scores the full predicted distribution of regression models
with proper scoring rules (<a href="#A3.SS7" title="C.7 ScoringBench ‣ Appendix C More Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.7</span></a>).</p>
</div></li>
<li id="S2.I1.i5" class="ltx_item" style="list-style-type:none;"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span> 
<div id="S2.I1.i5.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.I1.i5.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S2.I1.i5.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Task-specific harnesses.</span> The gains carry over to task-specific harnesses: TabPFN-Rel built on <span id="S2.I1.i5.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Plus</span> ranks first on RelArena-<math id="S2.I1.i5.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\alpha" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>α</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math> for relational data (<a href="#S2.SS5" title="2.5 RelArena-𝛼 ‣ 2 Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2.5</span></a>), and the general <span id="S2.I1.i5.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span>
checkpoint, with no time-series finetuning, beats the time-series-specific TabPFN-TS-3 on fev-bench (<a href="#A3.SS6" title="C.6 fev-bench ‣ Appendix C More Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.6</span></a>).</p>
</div></li>
</ul>
</div>
<div id="S2.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.p2.1" class="ltx_p">Together, <span id="S2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span>, <span id="S2.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Fast</span> and <span id="S2.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Thinking</span> improve on <span id="S2.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> at every time budget. <span id="S2.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> itself runs up to <math id="S2.p2.m1" class="ltx_math_unparsed" alttext="2\times" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0.222em">×</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">2\times</annotation></semantics></math> slower than <span id="S2.p2.1.6" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> on large training sets because of its doubled model width;
<span id="S2.p2.1.7" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Fast</span> is up to <math id="S2.p2.m2" class="ltx_math_unparsed" alttext="3\times" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo lspace="0.222em">×</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">3\times</annotation></semantics></math> faster than <span id="S2.p2.1.8" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> while still about 150 Elo points ahead on TabArena. See <a href="#S2.SS6" title="2.6 Inference benchmarks ‣ 2 Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2.6</span></a> for inference-speed measurements and details on how our <span id="S2.p2.1.9" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Plus</span> offering makes <span id="S2.p2.1.10" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> and <span id="S2.p2.1.11" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Fast</span> even faster.</p>
</div>
<section id="S2.SS1" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.1 </span>TabArena</h3>

<div id="S2.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.SS1.p1.1" class="ltx_p">TabArena <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib2" title="" class="ltx_ref">2</a>]</cite> has become increasingly competitive with the arrival of strong tabular foundation models such as TabFM <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib33" title="" class="ltx_ref">33</a>]</cite> and EXAONE-Tabular <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib34" title="" class="ltx_ref">34</a>]</cite>. Indeed, every method in the current top ten is either a tabular foundation model in its default configuration or a highly optimized AutoML system.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.SS1.p2.1" class="ltx_p"><a href="#S2.F2" title="In 2.1 TabArena ‣ 2 Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> shows the Pareto frontier for Elo and total fit-and-predict time; <a href="#A3.F13" title="In What drives improvability differences? ‣ C.1.3 Additional results on TabArena ‣ C.1 Additional details on TabArena ‣ Appendix C More Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">13</span></a> shows the corresponding improvability frontier. Against a strong set of recent baselines, <span id="S2.SS1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Thinking</span>, <span id="S2.SS1.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> and <span id="S2.SS1.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Fast</span> form the Pareto frontier: at every time budget one of them reaches the highest Elo. <span id="S2.SS1.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> beats TabFM by 84 Elo points using one-tenth of its total time per sample, and outperforms AutoGluon 1.6 extreme <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib14" title="" class="ltx_ref">14</a>]</cite> by 130 Elo points in a fifth of the time. <a href="#A3.SS1.SSS4" title="C.1.4 How the time axis of the TabArena Pareto frontier is computed ‣ C.1 Additional details on TabArena ‣ Appendix C More Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.1.4</span></a> describes how the time axis is measured.</p>
</div>
<figure id="S2.F2" class="ltx_figure"><object type="image/svg+xml" data="2609.17895v2/pareto_front_elo_vs_time_total_joint_setC__20260909-tabarena-rtxpro6000-wrapperfix.svg" id="S2.F2.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" style="aspect-ratio:476/279;" width="476" height="279"></object>
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S2.F2.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 2</span>: </span><span id="S2.F2.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:90%;">TabArena Elo against total time.<span id="S2.F2.5.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> Total time is the fit time per 1,000 training rows plus the prediction time per 1,000 test rows, median over the 51 datasets, on a log scale; <a href="#A3.SS1.SSS4" title="C.1.4 How the time axis of the TabArena Pareto frontier is computed ‣ C.1 Additional details on TabArena ‣ Appendix C More Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.1.4</span></a> describes the measurement.</span></span></figcaption>
</figure>
</section>
<section id="S2.SS2" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.2 </span>BeyondArena</h3>

<div id="S2.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.SS2.p1.1" class="ltx_p">BeyondArena <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib3" title="" class="ltx_ref">3</a>]</cite> tests whether tabular models generalize beyond the small, IID settings emphasized by standard benchmarks. It includes datasets with different training and test distributions, spanning grouped splits (predicting on data from a subgroup unseen during training, e.g. a new country or a new hospital), and temporal splits (predicting on test samples observed later in time than the training samples). BeyondArena also contains tiny to million-row datasets; low and high-dimensional tables; and text and high-cardinality features. Earlier TFMs performed strongly on small IID datasets but remained behind tuned conventional models in several of these harder regimes.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.SS2.p2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S2.SS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> establishes a new state of the art on the full BeyondArena core (<a href="#S2.F3" title="In 2.2 BeyondArena ‣ 2 Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>). It ranks first overall and improves over <span id="S2.SS2.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> in every slice, with particularly strong results on IID, high-dimensional, high-cardinality, and text data. The confidence intervals are wide for some of the smaller slices, so their exact ordering should be interpreted cautiously. Tuned and ensembled MLPs retain the highest performance on grouped, temporal, and large datasets, but <span id="S2.SS2.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> substantially narrows these gaps. The temporal (and to a lesser extent the grouped) subset of BeyondArena is confounded with size. When restricted to BeyondArena non-large (datasets with up to 100K rows), TabPFN-3.5 matches the best baselines for grouped and temporal datasets. See Appendix <a href="#A3.SS2.SSS3" title="C.2.3 Exposing the group identifier on label-per-sample grouped tasks ‣ C.2 Additional results on BeyondArena ‣ Appendix C More Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.2.3</span></a> for more details on how we evaluate models on grouped datasets, which differs slightly from the current BeyondArena procedure.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.SS2.p3.1" class="ltx_p">In Appendix <a href="#A3.SS2.SSS2" title="C.2.2 Comparison to recent TFMs ‣ C.2 Additional results on BeyondArena ‣ Appendix C More Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.2.2</span></a>, we compare to more recent TFMs that are absent from the official BeyondArena results and cannot run on the full benchmark. This comparison confirms its lead on the evaluated subset.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.SS2.p4.1" class="ltx_p">Finally, we also show the results of TabPFN-3.5-Thinking and TabPFN-3.5-Fast on BeyondArena in Figure <a href="#S2.F3" title="Figure 3 ‣ 2.2 BeyondArena ‣ 2 Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>. TabPFN-3.5-Thinking improves on TabPFN-3.5 by 20 Elo points, while TabPFN-3.5-Fast is 3x faster and scores only 40 Elo points lower.</p>
</div>
<figure id="S2.F3" class="ltx_figure"><object type="image/svg+xml" data="2609.17895v2/beyondarena-splits-per-method-with-fast-with-thinking__20260903-beyondarena-4554301a8c-tabpfn3gid.svg" id="S2.F3.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" style="aspect-ratio:476/152;" width="476" height="152"></object>
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S2.F3.6" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 3</span>: </span><span id="S2.F3.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:90%;">Performance on BeyondArena core.<span id="S2.F3.7.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> Elo scores compare <span id="S2.F3.7.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> with <span id="S2.F3.7.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span>, TabICLv2, and the strongest tuned and ensembled GBDT and MLP baselines, overall and by data slice. Error bars show bootstrap confidence intervals. The labels of the Elo scores are rounded to the nearest multiple of 10. The group identifier is exposed to the TabPFN models on label-per-sample grouped datasets; see <a href="#A3.SS2.SSS3" title="C.2.3 Exposing the group identifier on label-per-sample grouped tasks ‣ C.2 Additional results on BeyondArena ‣ Appendix C More Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.2.3</span></a>.</span></span></figcaption>
</figure>
<figure id="S2.F4" class="ltx_figure"><object type="image/svg+xml" data="2609.17895v2/beyondarena-splits-per-method-with-fast-with-thinking-nolarge__20260903-beyondarena-4554301a8c-tabpfn3gid.svg" id="S2.F4.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" style="aspect-ratio:476/177;" width="476" height="177"></object>
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S2.F4.5" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 4</span>: </span><span id="S2.F4.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:90%;">BeyondArena without large tables.<span id="S2.F4.6.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> <span id="S2.F4.6.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> leads overall and across the reported data regimes, while matching the strongest baseline on grouped data. Error bars show bootstrap confidence intervals. The labels of the Elo scores are rounded to the nearest multiple of 10. The group identifier is exposed to the TabPFN models on label-per-sample grouped datasets; see <a href="#A3.SS2.SSS3" title="C.2.3 Exposing the group identifier on label-per-sample grouped tasks ‣ C.2 Additional results on BeyondArena ‣ Appendix C More Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.2.3</span></a>.</span></span></figcaption>
</figure>
</section>
<section id="S2.SS3" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.3 </span>STRABLE</h3>

<div id="S2.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.SS3.p1.1" class="ltx_p">Enterprise tables frequently contain product names, addresses, job titles, and identifiers alongside numeric columns.
STRABLE <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib4" title="" class="ltx_ref">4</a>]</cite> is the largest benchmark of tabular learning with strings, comprising 108 datasets drawn from diverse application fields. We compare against the results published in the official paper, without re-running baselines; consequently, models released after the paper are absent from the comparison. Since TF-IDF was the strongest string encoding for STRABLE, we use it for models without native text handling, including <span id="S2.SS3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> and <span id="S2.SS3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Fast</span>; models with native text handling <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib39" title="" class="ltx_ref">39</a>, <a href="#bib.bib36" title="" class="ltx_ref">36</a>, <a href="#bib.bib40" title="" class="ltx_ref">40</a>]</cite>, as well as <span id="S2.SS3.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Plus</span> and <span id="S2.SS3.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Thinking</span>, receive the raw table.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.SS3.p2.1" class="ltx_p">Figure <a href="#S2.F5.sf1" title="Figure 5(a) ‣ Figure 5 ‣ 2.3 STRABLE ‣ 2 Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5(a)</span></a> shows that the <span id="S2.SS3.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> family dominates the benchmark, with all variants significantly outperforming prior baselines. Appendix <a href="#A3.SS3" title="C.3 Additional Results on STRABLE ‣ Appendix C More Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.3</span></a> shows the leaderboard including variants using the embedding model <span id="S2.SS3.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">e5-small-v2</span> <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib41" title="" class="ltx_ref">41</a>]</cite> for string encoding.</p>
</div>
<figure id="S2.F5" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">
<figure id="S2.F5.sf1" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center"><object type="image/svg+xml" data="2609.17895v2/elo_strable_tfidf.svg" id="S2.F5.sf1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_square" style="aspect-ratio:476/457;" width="476" height="457"></object>
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S2.F5.sf1.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">(a)</span> </span><span id="S2.F5.sf1.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">STRABLE with TF-IDF: 108 datasets, 3 folds each.</span></figcaption>
</figure></div><div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">
<figure id="S2.F5.sf2" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center"><object type="image/svg+xml" data="2609.17895v2/elo_multabench.svg" id="S2.F5.sf2.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_square" style="aspect-ratio:476/457;" width="476" height="457"></object>
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S2.F5.sf2.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">(b)</span> </span><span id="S2.F5.sf2.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">MulTaBench: 40 datasets, 5 folds each.</span></figcaption>
</figure></div></div>
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S2.F5.5" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 5</span>: </span><span id="S2.F5.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:90%;">Elo on STRABLE and MulTaBench<span id="S2.F5.6.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium">, over the results published with each benchmark. The <span id="S2.F5.6.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> family significantly outperforms all published baselines on both.</span></span></figcaption>
</figure>
</section>
<section id="S2.SS4" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.4 </span>MulTaBench</h3>

<div id="S2.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.SS4.p1.1" class="ltx_p">In many high-impact domains, tabular learning problems are inherently multimodal, combining structured numerical features with unstructured inputs like free-text and images. MulTaBench <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib5" title="" class="ltx_ref">5</a>]</cite> benchmarks this setting across 40 datasets: 20 with an image column and 20 with free-text columns. We compare against the results published in the official paper without re-running baselines; consequently, models released after the paper are absent from the comparison. In the official benchmark, baselines lacking native text support use frozen <span id="S2.SS4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">e5-small-v2</span> <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib41" title="" class="ltx_ref">41</a>]</cite> embeddings, and baselines lacking image support rely on frozen <span id="S2.SS4.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">DINOv3-small</span> <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib42" title="" class="ltx_ref">42</a>]</cite> embeddings. Both of them are then reduced to 30 PCA <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib43" title="" class="ltx_ref">43</a>]</cite> components. To ensure a fair comparison, we evaluate <span id="S2.SS4.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> and <span id="S2.SS4.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Fast</span> using the same preprocessing.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.SS4.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.SS4.p2.1" class="ltx_p">Figure <a href="#S2.F5.sf2" title="Figure 5(b) ‣ Figure 5 ‣ 2.3 STRABLE ‣ 2 Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5(b)</span></a> also includes <span id="S2.SS4.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Plus</span> and <span id="S2.SS4.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Thinking</span>, which process the raw text columns directly (Section <a href="#S3.SS4" title="3.4 TabPFN-3.5-Plus and Thinking mode ‣ 3 Model Design and Inference" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.4</span></a>) while relying on the frozen embeddings for image features. As shown in the figure, the <span id="S2.SS4.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> family significantly outperforms all prior published methods. Appendix <a href="#A3.SS4" title="C.4 Additional Results on MulTaBench ‣ Appendix C More Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.4</span></a> details the text-only half of the benchmark, where the performance gap between our models and the baselines widens further, thanks to our improved text support.</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="S2.SS5" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.5 </span>RelArena-<math id="S2.SS5.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\alpha" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>α</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math></h3>

<figure id="S2.F6" class="ltx_figure"><object type="image/svg+xml" data="2609.17895v2/relarena-elo-joint-model_and_system.svg" id="S2.F6.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" style="aspect-ratio:476/248;" width="476" height="248"></object>
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S2.F6.7" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 6</span>: </span><span id="S2.F6.8" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:90%;">Elo scores on the model + system leaderboard over 21 RelArena-<math id="S2.F6.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\alpha" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>α</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math> tasks:<span id="S2.F6.8.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> Entries marked as systems (hatched) comply with RelArena-<math id="S2.F6.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\alpha" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>α</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math>’s data states and evaluation regime but not with its standardized tuning regime (see <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citet"><a href="#bib.bib6" title="" class="ltx_ref">Hayler et al. [6]</a></cite> for further details). We observe that updating the base model from <span id="S2.F6.8.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3-Plus</span> to <span id="S2.F6.8.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Plus</span> improves performance by nearly 90 Elo points. We also preview our latest (internal) version of TabPFN-Rel, co-developed with <span id="S2.F6.8.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span>, which yields an additional 36 Elo-point improvement.</span></span></figcaption>
</figure>
<div id="S2.SS5.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.SS5.p1.1" class="ltx_p">As observed in previous work <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib1" title="" class="ltx_ref">1</a>]</cite>, improvements in the base model tend to translate directly into stronger downstream performance on relational tasks through the TabPFN-Rel harness <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib6" title="" class="ltx_ref">6</a>, <a href="#bib.bib1" title="" class="ltx_ref">1</a>]</cite>. <a href="#S2.F6" title="In 2.5 RelArena-𝛼 ‣ 2 Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a> illustrates this on the novel RelArena-<math id="S2.SS5.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\alpha" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>α</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math> benchmark <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib6" title="" class="ltx_ref">6</a>]</cite>, which enables reproducible and comparable evaluation on RelBench v1 entity-level tasks <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib44" title="" class="ltx_ref">44</a>]</cite>. Using TabPFN-3.5-Plus, TabPFN-Rel ranks first on both the models and model+systems leaderboards of RelArena-<math id="S2.SS5.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\alpha" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>α</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math>, outperforming the RT-PluRel system <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib45" title="" class="ltx_ref">45</a>, <a href="#bib.bib46" title="" class="ltx_ref">46</a>, <a href="#bib.bib47" title="" class="ltx_ref">47</a>]</cite> while requiring significantly less runtime.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.SS5.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.SS5.p2.1" class="ltx_p">The comparison includes RT-PluRel <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib45" title="" class="ltx_ref">45</a>, <a href="#bib.bib47" title="" class="ltx_ref">47</a>]</cite>, KurveRSC <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib48" title="" class="ltx_ref">48</a>]</cite>,
GraphSAGE <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib49" title="" class="ltx_ref">49</a>, <a href="#bib.bib44" title="" class="ltx_ref">44</a>]</cite>, RelGT <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib50" title="" class="ltx_ref">50</a>]</cite>, RDBLearn <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib51" title="" class="ltx_ref">51</a>]</cite>, and
RelGNN <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib52" title="" class="ltx_ref">52</a>]</cite>, alongside the released TabPFN-Rel variants <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib6" title="" class="ltx_ref">6</a>, <a href="#bib.bib1" title="" class="ltx_ref">1</a>]</cite>
and the global and per-entity constant predictors.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.SS5.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.SS5.p3.1" class="ltx_p">Building on these results, we continue to co-develop the TabPFN-Rel harness alongside the TabPFN base models. We additionally preview the latest internal version of the harness, which, when combined with TabPFN-3.5-Plus, improves performance by a further 36 Elo points, as also shown in <a href="#S2.F6" title="In 2.5 RelArena-𝛼 ‣ 2 Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a>. We co-developed the newer versions of the TabPFN-Rel harness with TabPFN-3.5-Plus. Results with open-source checkpoints and with Thinking mode are work in progress.</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="S2.SS6" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.6 </span>Inference benchmarks</h3>

<figure id="S2.F7" class="ltx_figure"><object type="image/svg+xml" data="2609.17895v2/inference-overview-100-columns__20260903-rtx-pro-6000-tabpfn-8b35121__20260903-kvcache-rtx-pro-6000-tabpfn-8b35121.svg" id="S2.F7.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" style="aspect-ratio:476/157;" width="476" height="157"></object>
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S2.F7.6" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 7</span>: </span><span id="S2.F7.7" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Inference speed at 100 columns and 1,024 test rows absolute (left) and relative to <span id="S2.F7.7.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> (middle). Dashed lines are the same checkpoints behind TabPFN-Plus. Right: predicting 1 or
100 test rows against a KV cache built over the training rows. On a single test row, <span id="S2.F7.7.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> matches the speed of <span id="S2.F7.7.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span>.</span></figcaption>
</figure>
<div id="S2.SS6.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.SS6.p1.1" class="ltx_p">We benchmark our new models <span id="S2.SS6.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> and <span id="S2.SS6.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Fast</span> against <span id="S2.SS6.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> on one NVIDIA RTX
PRO 6000 Blackwell (96 GB).
We run a single estimator, measure the model forward time, and multiply by the checkpoint’s default number of
estimators (8 for <span id="S2.SS6.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> and <span id="S2.SS6.p1.1.5" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span>, 4 for <span id="S2.SS6.p1.1.6" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Fast</span>), see <a href="#S2.F7" title="In 2.6 Inference benchmarks ‣ 2 Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a>. As the number of training rows grows from 1k to 1M, <span id="S2.SS6.p1.1.7" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> runs up to <math id="S2.SS6.p1.m1" class="ltx_math_unparsed" alttext="2\times" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0.222em">×</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">2\times</annotation></semantics></math> slower than <span id="S2.SS6.p1.1.8" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> due to the increased number of attention heads. Our optimized <span id="S2.SS6.p1.1.9" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Plus</span>, however, reduces the model’s latency and only has <math id="S2.SS6.p1.m2" class="ltx_math_unparsed" alttext="1.25\times" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mn>1.25</mn><mo lspace="0.222em">×</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1.25\times</annotation></semantics></math> the latency of <span id="S2.SS6.p1.1.10" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> due to faster FP8 attention <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib53" title="" class="ltx_ref">53</a>]</cite>.
<span id="S2.SS6.p1.1.11" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Fast</span> is up to 3 times faster than <span id="S2.SS6.p1.1.12" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> and the optimized Plus variant can run in a quarter of the time. All scale quadratically in the number of training rows for datasets larger than 128k rows.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.SS6.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.SS6.p2.1" class="ltx_p">Like our previous models, <span id="S2.SS6.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> also supports cached inference for sub-second predictions.
The expensive computations on the training rows are done only once and only the relevant tensors are cached.
The models cache the per-block inducing states produced by the feature
distribution embedder, the keys and values of the ICL self-attention blocks, and the inputs to the many-class decoder. For the latter, <span id="S2.SS6.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> caches the final ICL layer’s train embeddings.
We optimized this and <span id="S2.SS6.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> directly caches the input keys of the many-class decoder, reducing cache size as well as computation.
The only additional new structure that needs caching for <span id="S2.SS6.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> is for the ECDF features, for which we use a bucketed version to decouple its cache size from the number of training rows.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.SS6.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S2.SS6.p3.1" class="ltx_p">Overall, the size of the cache remains largely unchanged between <span id="S2.SS6.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> and <span id="S2.SS6.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> even though the parameter count roughly quadrupled and prediction quality improved substantially. This is crucial for online inference, where a single test example is scored against the training context. This online use-case is bound by the memory bandwidth of moving the cache (and the weights). As we see in <a href="#S2.F7" title="In 2.6 Inference benchmarks ‣ 2 Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a> for a single test row, the cached predict times between <span id="S2.SS6.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> and <span id="S2.SS6.p3.1.4" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> align exactly, whereas <span id="S2.SS6.p3.1.5" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Fast</span> is around three times faster.</p>
</div>
</section>
</section>
<section id="S3" class="ltx_section">
<h2 class="ltx_title ltx_title_section"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">3 </span>Model Design and Inference</h2>

<div id="S3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S3.p1.1" class="ltx_p">This section describes the changes from <span id="S3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> to <span id="S3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span>: revised per-cell encodings, an architecture optimized for joint multitask training, and a doubled model width (<a href="#S3.SS1" title="3.1 Architecture ‣ 3 Model Design and Inference" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.1</span></a>), simpler preprocessing (<a href="#S3.SS2" title="3.2 Preprocessing ‣ 3 Model Design and Inference" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.2</span></a>), and a synthetic prior tuned to high-cardinality, wide, and grouped data (<a href="#S3.SS3" title="3.3 Synthetic Prior ‣ 3 Model Design and Inference" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.3</span></a>).
<a href="#S3.SS4" title="3.4 TabPFN-3.5-Plus and Thinking mode ‣ 3 Model Design and Inference" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.4</span></a> introduces <span id="S3.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Plus</span> and its Thinking mode.</p>
</div>
<section id="S3.SS1" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.1 </span>Architecture</h3>

<div id="S3.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S3.SS1.p1.1" class="ltx_p">The architecture of <span id="S3.SS1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> retains
<span id="S3.SS1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> ’s <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib1" title="" class="ltx_ref">1</a>]</cite> overall design while revising several key components, summarized below.</p>
</div>
<div id="S3.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S3.SS1.p2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S3.SS1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Per-cell Encodings.</span> Cells of the table are embedded in three stages: per-cell preprocessing, feature grouping, and group embedding (<a href="#S3.F8" title="In 3.1 Architecture ‣ 3 Model Design and Inference" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">8</span></a>). We inherit the missing/infiniteness indicators and the feature grouping from <span id="S3.SS1.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> and add two more signals: first, the standardized values are encoded with Fourier features, inspired by TabFM <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib33" title="" class="ltx_ref">33</a>]</cite>, rather than projected only linearly: each value is multiplied by a bank of learned frequencies and passed through sine and cosine, which captures small differences better than a linear projection of the raw number. This is particularly advantageous for modeling ordinal-encoded categorical variables with high cardinality.
Second, we introduce in-context <em id="S3.SS1.p2.1.3" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">Empirical Cumulative Distribution Function (ECDF)</em> features: each cell is ranked against the cell values of the training rows of its own column, and the resulting <math id="S3.SS1.p2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="u\in[0,1]" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">u\in[0,1]</annotation></semantics></math> is expanded into low-frequency sine and cosine terms. This places a value in its column’s distribution rather than on its raw scale: midranks are unchanged by any strictly increasing transformation of a column, so a skewed feature and its logarithm yield identical ECDF terms, and a heavy tail that standard scaling crushes against the <math id="S3.SS1.p2.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\pm 100" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo>±</mo><mn>100</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\pm 100</annotation></semantics></math> clip is spread evenly over <math id="S3.SS1.p2.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="[0,1]" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">[0,1]</annotation></semantics></math>. As in <span id="S3.SS1.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span>, the standardized values are also passed through, so the exact magnitudes survive alongside the two new encodings. The target embedding is unchanged from <span id="S3.SS1.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span>: a learned embedding per class and a linear projection for regression targets.</p>
</div>
<figure id="S3.F8" class="ltx_figure"><object type="image/svg+xml" data="2609.17895v2/cell-encoding-flow.svg" id="S3.F8.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_square" style="aspect-ratio:476/456;" width="476" height="456"></object>
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S3.F8.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 8</span>: </span><span id="S3.F8.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:90%;">Per-cell encoding in <span id="S3.F8.5.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span>.<span id="S3.F8.5.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> B is the batch size, R and C the numbers of rows and columns, E = 128 the cell embedding width; each group spans G = 3 columns, the Fourier encoder uses F = 32 learned frequencies, and the ECDF encoder K = 4 harmonics. Preprocessing (top) records a missing/infinite
indicator before imputing with the training-column mean, then derives two signals from the imputed
value: a standard-scaled value and an in-context ECDF rank <math id="S3.F8.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="u\in[0,1]" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">u\in[0,1]</annotation></semantics></math> obtained by midranking
against the training rows of the same column. Feature grouping (middle) collects the <math id="S3.F8.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G=3" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>G</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G=3</annotation></semantics></math>
columns at circular offsets <math id="S3.F8.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="2^{j}\bmod C" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>j</mi></msup><mo>mod</mo><mi>C</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">2^{j}\bmod C</annotation></semantics></math> for all three signals, giving <math id="S3.F8.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="3G" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>G</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">3G</annotation></semantics></math> channels per cell.
Group embedding (bottom) encodes the scaled values with learnable Fourier features summed over the
<math id="S3.F8.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>G</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G</annotation></semantics></math> group positions, while a linear metadata encoder reads all <math id="S3.F8.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="3G" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>G</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">3G</annotation></semantics></math> channels: the scaled values
passed through, the missing/infinite indicators, and the ECDF ranks expanded into low-frequency
sine and cosine terms. The two encodings are summed and layer-normalised into the feature-group
embedding.</span></span></figcaption>
</figure>
<div id="S3.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S3.SS1.p3.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S3.SS1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Multitask Model.</span> We modified our model to be trained jointly on classification and regression, with the task type supplied as an input. Only the label encoder and the output head are task-specific; the cell encoder, per-column distribution embedder, feature aggregator and in-context transformer are shared. Classification is read out by attention over the one-hot training labels, regression by a predicted distribution over fixed bins. One run therefore yields a single checkpoint covering both tasks, reducing training cost and simplifying the inference stack. To keep training stable across the two tasks and the larger model width, we also added some new normalization layers throughout the network. Specifically, we added QK-norm (an RMSNorm on queries and keys) in the attention blocks, a LayerNorm after the input encoding, and an RMSNorm before the task-specific head.</p>
</div>
<div id="S3.SS1.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S3.SS1.p4.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S3.SS1.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Increased Capacity.</span>
We widen the in-context transformer from 512 to 1024 dimensions compared to <span id="S3.SS1.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span> by using eight CLS aggregation tokens per row instead of four. The attention head count doubles with it, from 8 to 16, so the per-head dimension stays at 64, and test rows still attend through a single 64-dimensional key/value head.
Doubling the dimension quadruples the parameter count in the linear layers, which dominate the overall parameter count. However, for large datasets (many rows), the inference time only doubles, as it is linear in the width rather than the parameter count.
Furthermore, the grouped-query attention for the test rows ensures that the size of the KV cache, and thus the cached-prediction time remains largely unchanged compared to <span id="S3.SS1.p4.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span>, at significantly improved accuracy, see <a href="#S2.SS6" title="2.6 Inference benchmarks ‣ 2 Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2.6</span></a>.</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="S3.SS2" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.2 </span>Preprocessing</h3>

<div id="S3.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S3.SS2.p1.1" class="ltx_p">Unlike previous TabPFN versions, <span id="S3.SS2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> does not rely on additional feature transformations such as quantile transformations or robust scaling, and does not augment the dataset with singular value decomposition (SVD) components. The improved cell encodings, described above and shown in <a href="#S3.F8" title="In 3.1 Architecture ‣ 3 Model Design and Inference" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">8</span></a>, provide a sufficiently expressive representation of each cell for the model to handle diverse datasets natively.
We still use multiple estimators to form a final prediction: by default, <span id="S3.SS2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> aggregates eight and <span id="S3.SS2.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Fast</span> four estimators that operate on distinct permutations of the dataset.</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="S3.SS3" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.3 </span>Synthetic Prior</h3>

<div id="S3.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S3.SS3.p1.1" class="ltx_p">Our synthetic data generation pipeline closely follows TabPFN-3 <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib1" title="" class="ltx_ref">1</a>]</cite>. We make some general improvements to diversity and scalability, alongside taking inspiration from the TabICLv2 prior <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib32" title="" class="ltx_ref">32</a>]</cite>. Furthermore, we put a specific emphasis on generating more realistic datasets that exhibit high-cardinality categorical variables in conjunction with our encoding improvements detailed in <a href="#S3.SS1" title="3.1 Architecture ‣ 3 Model Design and Inference" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.1</span></a>. Moreover, we adjusted our prior to generate more realistic datasets with large feature counts and grouped data, where the test set is from a different group than the train set. <a href="#S2.F4" title="In 2.2 BeyondArena ‣ 2 Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a> shows the effect on the grouped, high-feature-count, and high-cardinality slices of BeyondArena.</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="S3.SS4" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.4 </span><span id="S3.SS4.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Plus</span> and Thinking mode</h3>

<div id="S3.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S3.SS4.p1.1" class="ltx_p">On top of <span id="S3.SS4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span>, which we release open-source, our API and enterprise deployments provide access
to <span id="S3.SS4.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Plus</span> and <span id="S3.SS4.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Thinking</span>. These variants are fully compatible with the open-source <span id="S3.SS4.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span> interface and can be used as a drop-in replacement, while offering additional capabilities.</p>
</div>
<div id="S3.SS4.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S3.SS4.p2.1" class="ltx_p">While our open-source release now supports preprocessing of dates and text features, <span id="S3.SS4.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">TabPFN-3.5-Plus</span> further improves on multimodal data.
Furthermore, <span id="S3.SS4.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Plus</span> provides latency-optimized inference, most notably through FP8 attention that accelerates inference on datasets with many rows <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib53" title="" class="ltx_ref">53</a>]</cite>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S3.SS4.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S3.SS4.p3.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S3.SS4.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">TabPFN-3.5-Thinking</span> applies additional inference-time computation on top of
<span id="S3.SS4.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Plus</span> to push prediction quality further. Thinking mode composes with native multimodal
support, so a single call can handle mixed numerical, categorical, date, and text columns under the same
inference-time-compute regime. We emphasize that our Thinking mode achieves this strong performance
while relying only on <span id="S3.SS4.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5</span>, without using LLMs, real data, internet search, or any other model.</p>
</div>
<div id="S3.SS4.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S3.SS4.p4.1" class="ltx_p">These models substantially improve on <span id="S3.SS4.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span>-Plus and <span id="S3.SS4.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span>-Thinking <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib1" title="" class="ltx_ref">1</a>]</cite>. First, both benefit directly from the stronger base model. Second, we have significantly improved their implementation. Notably, <span id="S3.SS4.p4.1.3" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Thinking</span> is twelve times faster than <span id="S3.SS4.p4.1.4" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3</span>-Thinking, and <span id="S3.SS4.p4.1.5" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Plus</span> now comes with inference speed optimizations. The implementation details of <span id="S3.SS4.p4.1.6" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Plus</span> and <span id="S3.SS4.p4.1.7" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Thinking</span> are proprietary and are intentionally not described in this report.</p>
</div>
<div id="S3.SS4.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S3.SS4.p5.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S3.SS4.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Plus</span> and <span id="S3.SS4.p5.1.2" class="ltx_text">TabPFN-3.5-Thinking</span> are available through our API and through enterprise deployments including on-prem, Virtual Private Cloud (VPC) deployment on AWS SageMaker and Azure AI Foundry, and SAP AI Core; see <a href="#S4.SS1" title="4.1 Distribution ‣ 4 License and Availability" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4.1</span></a> and <a href="#S4" title="4 License and Availability" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a> for deployment options, licensing and access. Benchmark results are reported in <a href="#S2" title="2 Experimental Results" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>.</p>
</div>
</section>
</section>
<section id="S4" class="ltx_section">

<div id="S4.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S4.p1.1" class="ltx_p">Our open-source release has two parts under two licenses: the code, and the model weights.</p>
</div>
<section id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="ltx_paragraph">
<h5 class="ltx_title ltx_title_paragraph">Code.</h5>

<p id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1" class="ltx_p">Our open-source code, including the <span id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">tabpfn</span> package<span id="footnote1" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">1</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">1</sup>
